Robust Data Fusion via Subsampling
Jing Wang, HaiYing Wang, Kun Chen

Abstract
Data fusion and transfer learning are rapidly growing fields that enhance model performance for a target population by leveraging other related data sources or tasks. The challenges lie in the various potential heterogeneities between the target and external data, as well as various practical concerns that prevent a naïve data integration. We consider a realistic scenario where the target data is limited in size while the external data is large but contaminated with outliers; such data contamination, along with other computational and operational constraints, necessitates proper selection or subsampling of the external data for transfer learning. To our knowledge, transfer learning and subsampling under data contamination have not been thoroughly investigated. We address this gap by studying various transfer learning methods with subsamples of the external data, accounting for outliers deviating from the underlying true model due to arbitrary mean shifts. Two subsampling strategies are investigated: one aimed at reducing biases and the other at minimizing variances. Approaches to combine these strategies are also introduced to enhance the performance of the estimators. We provide non-asymptotic error bounds for the transfer learning estimators, clarifying the roles of sample sizes, signal strength, sampling rates, magnitude of outliers, and tail behaviors of model error distributions, among other factors. Extensive simulations show the superior performance of the proposed methods. Additionally, we apply our methods to analyze the risk of hard landings in A380 airplanes by utilizing data from other airplane types, demonstrating that robust transfer learning can improve estimation efficiency for relatively rare airplane types with the help of data from other types of airplanes. Keywords: Mean shifts; Non-asymptotic error bounds; Outliers; Transfer learning.



Department of Statistics, University of Connecticut

1
Introduction

Transfer learning and data fusion have gained significant attention in
statistics and machine learning in recent years

(
Rosenstein et al. 2005
;
Chen et al. 2015
;
Li et al. 2022
)
. A typical scenario
for transfer learning is that the target task has limited data but can be
potentially enhanced by a substantial amount of external data from one or many
sources. As such, transfer learning techniques can be particularly beneficial in
fields like engineering and medicine
(
Li et al. 2022
;
Jin et al. 2024
)
,
where collecting sufficient data for a specific task is costly or data is
collected from disparate sources and thus can be heterogeneous. For example, in
air safety studies, airplane hard landings can be analyzed with Quick Access
Recorder (QAR) data. However, QAR data for certain types of airplanes, such as
the Airbus A380, remains limited
(
Chen and Jin 2021
)
. Borrowing
information from other airplane types, such as the Airbus A320 and the Boeing
737, can potentially improve the prediction of hard landings for the Airbus
A380. In suicide risk studies with electronic health records (EHRs), transfer
learning and data fusion can be used to build more accurate models
for a local healthcare provider with limited data, when a separate and more
comprehensive EHR or claims database is available
(
Xu et al. 2022
)
.

However, external data can be a double-edged sword: naively utilizing all its
information could lead to negative transfer, where the resulting model performs
worse than using only the target data
(
Rosenstein et al. 2005
)
. This is
mainly due to potential inconsistencies and heterogeneity between the target and
the external sources, and various practical concerns prevent complete data
integration. In the modeling of hard landings, negative transfer could occur if
some flights or airplanes, such as the Boeing 737, exhibit substantially
different landing behaviors compared to typical Airbus A380 flights. In a
suicide risk study, negative transfer may occur if the external source cohort, e.g.,
patients in a statewide database, are very different from the target, e.g.,
patients at a children’s hospital.

The effective transfer of knowledge from external sources to the target relies
on exploring and utilizing their connections. One commonly adopted similarity
assumption is that the sources and the target share similar model parameters

(
Li et al. 2022
;
Tian and Feng 2023
;
Jin et al. 2024
)
, and the difference between the
parameters has to be sufficiently small to ensure a positive transfer. More
recently, an alternative assumption is that the target distribution can be
approximated by a mixture of source distributions without necessarily being
similar to each other

Hu et al. 2018
;
Mohri et al. 2019
. While this mixing assumption is much weaker than the
similarity assumption, it is less intuitive and does not apply when there are
only one or a few large sources. In either of the two approaches, a source is
modeled as a whole without considering within-source heterogeneity, thus
limiting their capability of handling many real-world scenarios.

In this study, we focus on a prototypical scenario where the target data is
limited in size, while the external data is large but contaminated with
outliers. This setup is practical and novel, and neither the aforementioned
similarity assumption nor the mixing assumption may hold. The data
contamination, along with other computational and operational constraints,
necessitates a new strategy for effective transfer learning.

Since anomalies in the external data could lead to significant estimation biases
and variances, we argue that data selection techniques are crucial for ensuring
the success of transfer learning. To achieve this goal, we utilize ideas from
another rapidly developing field, subsampling
(
Drineas et al. 2011
;
Ma et al. 2015
;
Wang et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2019
;
Ai et al. 2021
)
, to select
informative data for transfer learning. To the best of our
knowledge, existing subsampling and transfer learning literature seldom
considers data with outliers. On the other hand, it is a natural scenario that
the target data and a subset of the external source data share the same or similar
underlying models, and the large external dataset as a whole can be quite
heterogeneous.

We address the above gap by studying robust transfer learning methods with
subsamples of the external source data, accounting for outliers deviating from
the underlying true model due to arbitrary mean shifts. Our learning strategy
is summarized in Figure
1
. Two distinct subsampling strategies are
investigated: one aimed at reducing biases, referred to as “Target-guided
selection”, and the other at minimizing variances, referred to as
“Leverage-based random sampling” or simply “random sampling.” Our thorough
theoretical analysis quantifies the performance of
each transfer learning estimator in terms of bias and variance, as a function of
important structural parameters including sample sizes, magnitudes of outliers,
the sampling rate, tail behaviors of model errors, and others. Furthermore, we
consider methods to combine these strategies to enhance the performance of transfer,
corresponding to the “Robust data fusion” step that utilizes both target data
and sampled external data.

Figure 1
:
Diagram of robust transfer learning with subsampling.

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. Section
2

provides an overview of transfer learning and subsampling, along with a formal
definition of the problem setup. In Section
3
, we discuss random
sampling strategies for transfer learning and present their theoretical
properties. Section
4
then introduces a target-guided data
selection method, accompanied by theoretical analysis. Computational aspects,
including combined estimators and practical implementation issues, are addressed
in Section
5
. Section
6
presents numerical
experiments using both simulated and real-world data to validate our
approach. We conclude the paper in Section
7
. Detailed proofs
of all the theoretical results are provided in the supplementary material.

2
Transfer Learning under Source Contamination

2.1
Overview of Transfer Learning and Subsampling

We start by providing a brief review of transfer learning and
subsampling. Transfer learning has garnered significant interest in the
statistical community.
Chen et al. 2015
proposed a framework for transfer
learning in linear regression involving one target and one external data. They
introduced a fusion estimator that pools the target and external data, adding a
shrinkage penalty on the parameters of external data.
Li et al. 2022

investigated high-dimensional
linear regression with multiple external sources. Their algorithms include two
steps: first, train a fusion model with all available data; second, de-bias
using the target data with
\ell_{1}
penalization. This methods was extended to
generalized linear models in
Tian and Feng 2023
and high-dimensional
quantile regression in
Li et al. 2022
. All these works assumed a
similarity condition that
the target and the sources have models with similar parameters. Another more recent and
potentially weaker assumption is that the target model can be well approximated by a
mixture of the source models, under which group distributional robust optimization

(
Hu et al. 2018
)
and agnostic federated learning methods
(
Mohri et al. 2019
)
have been
developed for transfer learning.

Subsampling is another rapidly growing field.
Drineas et al. 2011
proposed to use
uniform sampling to accelerate least-squares approximation.
Ma and Sun 2015
and

Ma et al. 2015
studied leverage score subsampling for linear
regression from the statistical aspect.
Wang et al. 2018
proposed optimal
subsampling probabilities for
logistic regression, and
Ai et al. 2021
extended these methods to
generalized linear models. Besides random subsampling, deterministic selection
methods also are major focuses.
Wang et al. 2019
proposed an
information-based optimal subdata selection scheme named IBOSS, using ideas from
optimal design of experiments.
Cheng et al. 2020
extended IBOSS to
logistic regression,
and
Singh and Stufken 2023
developed deterministic data selection with a large
number of variables.

Despite extensive research on subsampling algorithms, few studies consider
subsampling in the presence of outliers. We aim to address this gap within
the context of regression with potential mean shifts.

She and Owen 2011
and
She and Chen 2017
investigated robust regression with
outlier detection using penalized mean-shift
models, connecting mean-shift penalization to robust regression,
particularly Huber regression
(
Huber 1983
)
when using
\ell_{1}

penalization. Theoretical properties of mean-shift models
were also studied in
Beyhum 2020
. From the perspective of robust
regression,
Sun et al. 2020
provided theoretical results for Huber
regression, which is equivalent to linear regression with mean shifts and
\ell_{1}

penalization.

2.2
Problem Setup & General Form of the Estimators

We begin with defining some notations. We use
\|\bm{v}\|_{p}
and
\|\bm{A}\|_{p}
to represent the

p
-norm of a vector
\bm{v}
and a matrix
\bm{A}
, respectively. For a fixed matrix
\bm{A}
, we
use notation
\|\bm{v}\|_{\bm{A},2}=\sqrt{\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{v}}
to represent the 2-norm according to
a matrix
\bm{A}
. We define
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
. For any two sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we use

a_{n}=O(b_{n})
and
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
to denote
|a_{n}/b_{n}|\leq C
for some positive constant

C
when
n
is large enough; we use
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
to denote
|a_{n}/b_{n}|\to 0
as

n\to\infty
.

We consider the scenario that we have data from a target and an external
source. The target data, denoted as
\mathcal{T}
, is with a relatively small sample size
n_{\mathcal{T}}
from the population of interest; the external source
data, denoted as
\Xi
, is with a relatively much larger sample size
n_{\Xi}
from a relevant yet
different population, thus subjecting to data contamination with respect to the target data.
Our main goal is properly utilize the source data to improve the estimation and
prediction for the target population. With some abuse of notations, we also use

\mathcal{T}=\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{T}}\}
to represent the target data index set and

\Xi=\{n_{\mathcal{T}}+1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{T}}+n_{\Xi}\}
to represent the external data index set.

To focus on the main idea, consider a linear regression model on the target:

y_{t}=\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta}_{0}+\varepsilon_{t},\qquad t\in\mathcal{T},

(1)

where
y_{t}
is the response,
\bm{x}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the covariate vector,

\bm{\beta}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the true coefficient vector of interest, and

\varepsilon_{t}
’s are independent and identically distributed
(i.i.d.) random errors with mean zero. For the “contaminated” source, we
assume a mean-shift model,

y_{e}=\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}_{0}+\gamma_{e}+\varepsilon_{e},\qquad e\in\Xi,

(2)

where
\bm{\beta}_{0}
remains the same as in (
1
),
\gamma_{e}
’s are
the subject-specific mean-shift parameters, and
\varepsilon_{e}
’s are i.i.d. random
errors with mean zero.
We introduce some notations to facilitate
the presentation. Let
\bm{X}_{\mathcal{T}}=(\bm{x}_{1}^{\top},\bm{x}_{2}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{x}_{t}^{\top},\ldots)^{\top}
and

\bm{y}_{\mathcal{T}}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{t},\ldots)^{\top}
for
t\in\mathcal{T}
. For the external data,
let

\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}=(\ldots,{\bm{x}_{e}}^{\top},\ldots)^{\top}
,
\bm{y}_{\Xi^{*}}=(\ldots,y_{e},\ldots)^{\top}
,

\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi^{*}}=(\ldots,\gamma_{e},\ldots)^{\top}
, and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{\Xi^{*}}=(\ldots,\varepsilon_{e},\ldots)^{\top}
, for
e\in\Xi^{*}
, where
\Xi^{*}
is a subset of index set
\Xi
. A
special case is when
\Xi^{*}=\Xi
where the matrices and vectors above represent
the full external data matrices and vectors.

A key aspect of the above setup is that the potential similarity and discrepancy
between the target model and the source model are characterized by the unknown
mean-shift terms (
\gamma_{e}
). The target and the source are more similar when,
for example, the true values of
\gamma_{e}
are sparse or of small magnitude, and
vise versa. This is drastically different from the typical “similarity
assumption” in transfer learning where the target and the source are assumed to
have exactly the same model structure with similar yet “controllably” different
regression coefficient vectors. Our setup is much more realistic and flexible in that the
connection between the target and the source is reflected at the individual
observation level. This allows the presence of arbitrary anomalies in the
source that may easily violate the similarity assumption. The potential value of
the source data can be flexibly controlled by some structural or distributional
assumptions on the mean-shift parameters; we defer detailed technical
discussions to Sections
3
and
4
.

It is clear that naively using all data to fit the regression model
in (
1
) can fail miserably: while observations in the source
with none or small biases (as reflected in the magnitude of the mean-shift parameters

\gamma_{e}
) can help reduce the estimation variance of
\bm{\beta}_{0}
, observations with large
biases may lead to a substantial estimation bias that overrides any potential gain.

To enable transfer learning, we propose to consider subsampling of the source
data, coupled with a weighted and regularized fusion learning with combined
data. Specifically, we will investigate several different strategies of
sampling/selecting a subset of
\Xi
, denoted as
\Xi^{*}
, that could potentially lead to a
better bias-variance trade-off and thus improve transfer learning. With the
target data and the selected source data, we will construct sampling weights to
account for subsampling and adopt regularized estimation to alleviate the impact
of the potential mean shifts of the selected source data.

As such, the transfer
learning estimators of our interest take the following general form:

(\hat{\bm{\beta}}^{\top},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\top})^{\top}=\arg\min_{\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi^{*}}}\left\{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}}w_{e}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}-\gamma_{e})^{2}+\lambda\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}}w_{e}P(\gamma_{e})\right\},

(3)

where
P(\cdot)
is an even penalty
function and
\lambda
is a tuning parameter. A special case
of (
3
) is when
\Xi^{*}=\Xi
and
w_{e}=1,e\in\Xi
; this estimator
fuses all the external source data with the target data, so we
refer it as the full-data transfer learning estimator.

The idea of mean-shift regularization has been adopted for outlier
detection and robust regression with a single data set
(
She and Owen 2011
, e.g.,)
.
It has been shown that the resulting estimator is equivalent to a class
of robust estimators, including Huber regression

(
She and Owen 2011
)
. The following proposition shows that this connection remains for the transfer learning
estimators in (
3
).

Proposition 1
.

For any
\lambda>0

and penalty function
P(\cdot)
, the
\hat{\bm{\beta}}

in (
3
) is the minimizer of

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}}w_{e}\mathcal{H}\left(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta};\lambda\right),

(4)

where
\mathcal{H}(z;\lambda)=2\int_{0}^{z}\psi(u;\lambda)\mathrm{d}u=2\int_{0}^{z}\left\{u-\Theta(u;\lambda)\right\}\mathrm{d}u
, and

\Theta(z;\lambda)=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}\{(z-\gamma)^{2}+\lambda P(\gamma)\}
.

Proposition
1
extends Proposition 4.1 of
She and Owen 2011
to a
more general scenario; the latter is a special
case when there is no target data set,
\Xi^{*}=\Xi
, and
w_{e}^{*}=1
for all

e\in\Xi
. The result shows that
\hat{\bm{\beta}}

defined in (
3
) can be obtained by
minimizing a robust loss function that involves
only
\bm{\beta}
. For example, if
P(\gamma)=2|\gamma|
, the
\ell_{1}
penalty, then

\Theta(z;\lambda)=(z-\lambda)I(z>\lambda)+(z+\lambda)I(z<-\lambda)
and

\mathcal{H}(z;\lambda)=z^{2}I(|z|\leq\lambda)+(2\lambda|z|-\lambda^{2})I(|z|>\lambda)
. The
resulting
\Theta(z;\lambda)
is the soft thresholding function and
\mathcal{H}(z;\lambda)
is Huber’s loss widely
used in robust statistics. Another important special case is

P(\gamma)=\gamma^{2}
, the
\ell_{2}
penalty, for which

\Theta(z;\lambda)=z/(1+\lambda)
and
\mathcal{H}(z;\lambda)=\lambda z^{2}/(1+\lambda)
. This is related to shrinkage estimator and ridge
regression. Although Proposition
1
holds for general penalty
functions, we focus on the fundamental
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
penalties.
Specifically, we specify

P(\gamma)=2\nu^{-1}|\gamma|^{\nu},

where
\nu=1
or
2
corresponds to the
\ell_{1}
or
\ell_{2}
penalty,
respectively.

2.3
Subsampling Strategies

With the form of the transfer learning estimators defined in (
3
), the main
puzzle is to determine
\Xi^{*}
, i.e., selecting data points from the external
source
\Xi
to balance the bias-variance trade-off. Figure
2
illustrates
three approaches of data selection: (a) leverage-based random sampling, (b) target-guided selection,
and (c) the optimal subsampling method under the A-optimality criterion
(OSMAC)
(
Ai et al. 2021
)
.

(a)

Leverage-based selection

(b)

Target-guided selection

(c)

OSMAC selection

Figure 2
:
Illustration of data selection strategies. Unbiased observations (
\gamma=0
)
that do not deviate away from the target data are represented by hollow
circles (
,

) scattering around the true
regression line (the red reference line). Biased observations
(
\gamma\neq 0
) that deviate away from the target data are represented by
the circles filled with “+” (

,

).
Selected data points are colored in green (

,

).

The leverage-based random sampling
(
Ma and Sun 2015
;
Ma et al. 2015
)
, as shown in
Figure
2
(a), relies
only on the covariates to construct subsampling probabilities, focusing on selecting
the most influential data points to reduce estimation variance. However, in the presence
of mean-shift outliers, leverage-based sampling may select biased points; as such, it alone may not be effective in reducing bias for transfer learning.

The target-guided selection, as illustrated in Figure
2
(b), aims
to select source data with less biases utilizing the target data. Specifically,
a natural estimator of
\gamma_{e}
, the amount of mean shift or bias in a source
data point, is
y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
, where
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is the
least-squares estimator of
\bm{\beta}_{0}
from fitting the target data. The procedure
then uses the order statistics of
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|
to select data
points with smaller biases. However, the performance of this method relies on
the quality of the target-only estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
, which may have a large
variance due to the small sample size of the target data. As such, the resulting
subsamples may not be effective in reducing the estimation variance.

Optimal subsampling is a widely used method to reduce the asymptotic
variance of the subsampling estimator
(
Ma et al. 2015
;
Wang et al. 2018
;
Ai et al. 2021
, see)
. Figure
2
(c) illustrates the selection by OSMAC

(
Ai et al. 2021
)
. Optimal subsampling probabilities are constructed under
the assumption of no outliers, and they are approximately proportional to

|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}_{0}|
. This suggests that the method favors the selection of the
outliers and may jeopardize transfer learning. We have conducted a simulation
study to further illustrate this; see Section
A.2
in the supplementary material. The
results show that OSMAC performs even worse than uniform sampling estimator and
the target-only estimator.

It now becomes intuitive that while optimal subsampling such as OSMAC may fail
miserably
with contaminated data, both random sampling and target-based selection are subject to a
bias-variance tradeoff. A thorough investigation of their behaviors sheds light on the
best strategies of conducting transfer learning with a contaminated and heterogeneous source.
In the sequel,
we shall thoroughly investigate the two general strategies, namely, random sampling and
target-guided selection, as well as their adaptive integration, in conjunction with
transfer learning.

3
Transfer Learning with Random Sampling

In this section, we study random sampling for
transfer learning. We describe the random sampling procedure in
Section
3.1
and present the theoretical property of the transfer
learning estimator based on the selected data. We then discuss optimal sampling
probabilities, leveraging the insights from our theoretical analysis in
Section
3.3
.

3.1
Poisson Subsampling and the Resulting Estimator

Non-uniform random sampling is widely used in the subsampling literature to
reduce variance, as it assigns different sampling probabilities to individual
data points, allowing for the selection of a more informative subset. In
alignment with this approach, we adopt non-uniform Poisson sampling, which is
frequently employed in subsampling due to its higher statistical efficiency
compared to sampling with replacement
(
Wang 2019
;
Wang et al. 2022
, see)
.
Let our objective be to sample a nominal size of
r=\rho n_{\Xi}
data points from
the set
\Xi
based on sampling probabilities
\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
, where
0<\pi_{e}\leq 1
and
\sum_{e\in\Xi}\pi_{e}=r
. The Poisson sampling algorithm proceeds
as follows:

Algorithm 1

Poisson subsampling

1:

For
e=1,...,n_{\Xi}
:

2:

Generate
u_{e}\sim U[0,1]
;

3:

if

u_{e}\leq\pi_{e}

then

4:

include
(y_{e},\bm{x}_{e})
and record
\pi_{e}
in the subsample;

5:

end

if

The actual subsample size
r^{*}
resulted from Algorithm
1
is
random with
\mathbb{E}(r^{*})=r
. Denote the index set
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}=\{e\in\Xi:\delta_{e}=1\}
,
where
\delta_{e}=I(u_{e}\leq\pi_{e})
. We propose a transfer learning estimator
through inverse probability weighting (IPW) based on
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
by taking
\Xi^{*}=\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
,

w_{e}=\rho\pi_{e}^{-1}
and
P(\gamma_{e})=2\nu^{-1}|\gamma_{e}|^{\nu}

in (
3
), which has the following form:

\displaystyle(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}^{\top},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}^{\top})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}}\left\{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}\frac{\rho(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}-\gamma_{e})^{2}}{\pi_{e}}+\lambda\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}\frac{2\rho|\gamma_{e}|^{\nu}}{\nu\pi_{e}}\right\}.

(5)

The IPW is a widely used technique in
subsampling literature
(
Ai et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2022
, e.g.,)
. Here, we
also use weighted penalties for robust estimation. From
Proposition
1
, we know that
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
in (
5
)
is the minimizer of

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}\frac{\rho\mathcal{H}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta};\lambda)}{\pi_{e}},

where
\mathcal{H}(z;\lambda)=z^{2}I(|t|\leq\lambda)+(2\lambda|z|-\lambda^{2})I(|z|>\lambda)
for the
\ell_{1}
penalty and

\mathcal{H}(z;\lambda)=\lambda z^{2}/(1+\lambda)
for the
\ell_{2}
penalty. Therefore, our IPW transfer learning estimator
is the minimizer of an objective function that
combines a least-squares
objective function on
\mathcal{T}
and a weighted robust objective function on
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
.

3.2
Theoretical Analysis

In this section, we investigate the theoretical aspects of random sampling, starting with
presenting some general assumptions.

Assumption 1
.

The covariate
\bm{x}
in both
\mathcal{T}
and

\Xi
satisfy that
\mathbb{P}(|\bm{u}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{x}}|\geq z)\leq 2e^{-z^{2}\|\bm{u}\|_{2}^{2}/C_{0}^{2}}
for all

z\in\mathbb{R}
and
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\tilde{\bm{x}}=\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}
,

\bm{\Sigma}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\top})
, and
C_{0}>0
is a constant.

Assumption 2
.

The error term
\varepsilon_{t},t\in\mathcal{T}
are i.i.d. satisfying

\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{t})=0
,
\mathbb{V}(\varepsilon_{t})=\sigma_{\mathcal{T}}^{2}
, and

\mathbb{E}(|\varepsilon_{t}|^{3})<\infty
; and the error term
\varepsilon_{e},e\in\Xi
are
i.i.d. with a symmetric density
f_{\varepsilon}(x)
, satisfying

\mathbb{E}(|\varepsilon_{e}|^{1+\alpha_{1}})<\infty
for some
0<\alpha_{1}\leq 1
.

Assumption 3
.

The subsampling probabilities satisfy that

\min_{e\in\Xi}\pi_{e}\geq c_{\pi}\rho
for some constant
c_{\pi}>0
.

Assumption
1
requires that the covariate distribution is
sub-Gaussian. A similar assumption is also used
in
Sun et al. 2020
for theoretical analysis of Huber regression. In our
framework, we allow the covariates in the target data and external data to
follow different distributions.
Assumption
2
permits different error distributions for the target and external
data. When
\alpha_{1}=1
, the error terms
\varepsilon_{e}
possess the
second-order moment, which forms a standard condition for establishing
asymptotic results for least-squares estimators. In contrast, when
\alpha_{1}<1
,
the error terms may not have a finite second-order moment, implying that

\varepsilon_{e}
exhibit heavy-tailed behavior, in contrast to the random errors
in
\mathcal{T}
. This allows the external data to have larger variation than the
source data even when there are no outliers.
Assumption
3
is widely adopted in subsampling literature

(
Yu et al. 2020
;
Wang et al. 2022
;
Zhang et al. 2024
, e.g.,)
.

We first define some notation to ease the presentation on non-asymptotic bounds
of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\varepsilon,\alpha_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}=\frac{1}{n_{\Xi}}\sum_{e\in\Xi}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{|\varepsilon_{e}|^{1+\alpha_{1}}}{\pi_{e}}\Big|\bm{x}_{e}\right),

(6)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\gamma,\alpha_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}=\frac{1}{n_{\Xi}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{O}}\frac{|\gamma_{e}|^{1+\alpha_{1}}}{\pi_{e}},\text{
and}

(7)

\displaystyle s_{\gamma,\alpha_{2}}=\frac{1}{n_{\Xi}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{O}}|\gamma_{e}|^{1-\alpha_{2}},

(8)

where
\mathcal{O}=\{e\in\Xi:\gamma_{e}\neq 0\}
.
We now present a non-asymptotic bound for
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with
\ell_{1}

penalization in the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
3
, for any
\varsigma>0
and

0<\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\leq 1
, the estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}

in (
5
) with
\nu=1
and

\lambda=\left\{(c_{\pi}r)/\varsigma\right\}^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{1}}}

satisfies that

\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}-\bm{\beta}_{0}\|_{\bm{\Sigma},2}\lesssim\frac{n_{\mathcal{T}}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}I_{\mathcal{T}}+\frac{r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}I_{\Xi}+\frac{r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}I_{\gamma},

with probability at least
1-Ce^{-\varsigma}
, if

\displaystyle n_{\mathcal{T}}\gtrsim e^{2\varsigma},

(9)

\displaystyle r\gtrsim\sigma_{\gamma,\alpha_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}\varsigma,\text{ and}

(10)

\displaystyle(n_{\mathcal{T}}+r)r^{\frac{-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}\gtrsim s_{\gamma,\alpha_{2}}\varsigma^{\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{1+\alpha_{1}}},

(11)

where

\displaystyle I_{\mathcal{T}}=\sigma_{\mathcal{T}}\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{n_{\mathcal{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},I_{\Xi}=(\sigma_{\varepsilon,\alpha_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}\vee 1)\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{r}\right)^{\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+\alpha_{1}}},\text{
and}

\displaystyle I_{\gamma}:=I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{var}}+I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}:=(\sigma_{\gamma,\alpha_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}\vee 1)\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{r}\right)^{\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}+s_{\gamma,\alpha_{2}}\left(\frac{c_{\pi}r}{\varsigma}\right)^{\frac{\alpha_{2}}{1+\alpha_{1}}},

and
C
is a constant that does not depend on
n_{\mathcal{T}}
or
n_{\Xi}
.

Theorem
1
applies to general forms of bias terms. However, the
specific structure of these bias terms influences both the conditions and
corresponding bounds in the theorem. To facilitate our discussion of
Theorem
1
, we introduce Assumption
4
, which
describes a mixed structure for the bias terms.

Assumption 4
.

The mean-shift parameters
\gamma_{e}
are i.i.d. with the same distribution of

\gamma=\tilde{\gamma}I(u\leq p_{\gamma})
, where
\tilde{\gamma}
is a random
variable,
u\sim Unif[0,1]
is independent of
\tilde{\gamma}
, and
0<p_{\gamma}\leq 1
bounds
the probability of
\gamma
being non-zero.

In Assumption
4
, the bias term
\gamma
is modeled as a mixture of
zero and a nonzero random variable
\tilde{\gamma}
, with the mixture rate
controlled by
p_{\gamma}
. If
p_{\gamma}<1
, there is a positive probability
that
\gamma_{e}=0
for some
e\in\mathcal{B}
; if
p_{\gamma}=1
, all data
points in
\mathcal{B}
deviate from the model for
\mathcal{S}
.

Conditions (
9
), (
10
), and (
11
) in
Theorem
1
require the sample sizes to be large enough for the
bound to hold with the exponential tail probability rate.
Condition (
9
) is a restriction for
n_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Under Assumptions
3
and
4
, Condition (
10
)
reduces to

r\gtrsim p_{\gamma}\mathbb{E}(|\tilde{\gamma}|^{1+\alpha_{1}})\varsigma
. This
indicates that the required subsample size from the external data is affected
by the both the outlier proportion
p_{\gamma}
and the average magnitude of the
outliers
\mathbb{E}(|\tilde{\gamma}|^{1+\alpha_{1}})
.
Condition (
11
) puts a restriction on both
n_{\mathcal{T}}
and
r
. When

\alpha_{2}=1
,
s_{\gamma,\alpha_{2}}
becomes the sample proportion of outliers in the
external data, and Condition (
11
) reduces to

(n_{\mathcal{T}}+r)r^{-1}\gtrsim p_{\gamma}
under Assumption
4
. This is
always satisfied since
p_{\gamma}<1
.

The error bound of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
in Theorem
1
can be decomposed
into three sources. The
I_{\mathcal{T}}
in the first term can be regarded as the
uncertainty due to the randomness of the target data. The convergence rate of this
term is of
1/\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}}
, which is the regular least-squares convergence
rate. The
I_{\Xi}
in the second term can be regarded as the uncertainty due to randomness of
the external data. If
\sigma_{\varepsilon,\alpha_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}
is bounded in
probability, the convergence rate of
I_{\Xi}
is

1/r^{\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}
, which is the same as the
convergence rate of Huber regression estimator discussed in
Sun et al. 2020
.
When the model error for the external data
\varepsilon_{\Xi}
is light-tailed with a finite second moment,
we can set
\alpha_{1}=1
, and the rate becomes
1/\sqrt{r}
. When
\varepsilon_{\Xi}
’s
distribution has a heavier tail, the convergence rate is slower than the
regular root rate, but
I_{\Xi}
still converges to 0. This means our method with

\ell_{1}
penalty is able to transfer useful information from outlier
contaminated external data with heavy-tailed model errors.

The
I_{\gamma}
in the third term is the error due to the biases in the
external data, and it contains two parts
I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{var}}
and

I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}
. The first part
I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{var}}
can
be regarded as the contribution of
\gamma_{e}
to the variance, and it converges
to zero at the same rate as
I_{\Xi}
if
\sigma_{\gamma,\alpha_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}
is
bounded in probability. The second part
I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}
can be
regraded as the contribution of
\gamma_{e}
to the bias, and it may not converge
to zero without strong assumption on
\gamma_{e}
. Note that under
Assumption
4
,
s_{\gamma,\alpha_{2}}\approx p_{\gamma}\mathbb{E}(|\tilde{\gamma}|^{1-\alpha_{2}})
. If the outliers in the external data
are sparse, i.e.,
p_{\gamma}
is small, we can take
\alpha_{2}=1
so that

s_{\gamma,\alpha_{2}}
is not affected by the magnitude of the outliers at
all. In this case,
I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}\approx p_{\gamma}(c_{\pi}r/\varsigma)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{1}}}
, and we need

p_{\gamma}=o\{(\varsigma/r)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{1}}}\}
for it to go toward zero. If
the outliers is not very sparse and
p_{\gamma}
is bounded away from zero, then we
have
I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}\approx\mathbb{E}(|\tilde{\gamma}|^{1-\alpha_{2}})(c_{\pi}r/\varsigma)^{\frac{\alpha_{2}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}
and need

\mathbb{E}(|\tilde{\gamma}|^{1-\alpha_{2}})=o\{(\varsigma/r)^{\frac{\alpha_{2}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}\}

in order to have
I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}
converge to zero. This requires
the magnitudes of the biases to be sufficiently small if there are too many
outliers in the external data.

Our non-asymptotic error bound in Theorem
1
is more general than
existing results in the literature. For example, when
\alpha_{2}=1
, the error
bound can be rewritten as

\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}-\bm{\beta}_{0}\|_{\bm{\Sigma},2}\lesssim\frac{n_{\mathcal{T}}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}n_{\mathcal{T}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}(r^{-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}\vee\lambda p_{\gamma}),

(12)

because
s_{\gamma,\alpha_{2}=1}=p_{\gamma}
and
\lambda=\{(c_{\pi}r)/\varsigma\}^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{1}}}
. Taking
n_{\mathcal{T}}=0
and
r=n_{\Xi}
,
the estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
reduces to an
\ell_{1}
-penalized mean-shift regression estimator,
and in this case, our error bound is similar
to the asymptotic error bound in
Beyhum 2020
, which is

O(n_{\Xi}^{-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}\vee\lambda p_{\gamma})
. Our result
holds even if
\varepsilon_{\Xi}
is very heavy tailed while
Beyhum 2020
requires

\varepsilon_{\Xi}
to be light-tailed. In addition,
Beyhum 2020
does
not cover the scenario that the outliers are not sparse.

Theorem
1
also indicates the
substantial difference of our study from the existing investigations in the
subsampling literature.
To illustrate this, we simplify the bound in Theorem
1
as

\displaystyle\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}-\bm{\beta}_{0}\|_{\bm{\Sigma},2}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}\varsigma}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}+\frac{r^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{1}}}\varsigma^{\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}+\frac{r^{1+\frac{\alpha_{2}}{1+\alpha_{1}}}}{(n_{\mathcal{T}}+r)\varsigma^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{1}}}}.

(13)

Note that the bound is a monotonically decreasing function of
n_{\mathcal{T}}
but not a
monotonic function of
r
. Therefore, increasing the subsample size of the
contaminated external data may not always increase the estimation efficiency of

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
, which is different from the results in existing subsampling
literature. For the transfer learning scenario we are considering, the subsample
size provides a bias-variance trade-off, and using the entire external data may
not result in the best performance of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
. This justifies the
importance of data selection in transfer learning.

Our analysis in Theorem
1
focuses specifically on the
\ell_{1}

penalization. A similar error decomposition holds for the estimator

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with the
\ell_{2}
penalization. The corresponding error bound is
expressed in the following theorem.

Theorem 2
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
3
, if

\sigma_{\varepsilon,2}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}<\infty
, then for any
\lambda>0
, the estimator defined
in (
5
) with
\nu=2
satisfies that

\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}-\bm{\beta}_{0}\|_{\bm{\Sigma},2}\lesssim\frac{n_{\mathcal{T}}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+c_{\lambda}r}I_{\mathcal{T}}+\frac{c_{\lambda}r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+c_{\lambda}r}I_{\Xi}+\frac{c_{\lambda}r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+c_{\lambda}r}I_{\gamma},

with probability at least
1-Ce^{-\varsigma}
, when
n_{\mathcal{T}}\gtrsim e^{2\varsigma}

and
\sqrt{r}\gtrsim\sigma_{\gamma,2}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}e^{\varsigma}
,
where
c_{\lambda}=\lambda/(1+\lambda)
,

\displaystyle I_{\mathcal{T}}=\sigma_{\mathcal{T}}\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{n_{\mathcal{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},I_{\Xi}=\sqrt{\sigma_{\varepsilon,1}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}}\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\text{ and }

\displaystyle I_{\gamma}:=I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{var}}+I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}:=\sqrt{\sigma_{\gamma,1}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}}\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+s_{\gamma,0}.

The error bound for
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with
\ell_{2}
penalization is comparable to
the one with
\ell_{1}
penalization described in
Theorem
1
. Similar to the
\ell_{1}
case, the error for

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with
\ell_{2}
penalization can be decomposed into three
components:
I_{\mathcal{T}}
represents the contribution of randomness from the target
data;
I_{\Xi}
captures the randomness from the external data; and

I_{\gamma}
is associated with the bias terms.

While the interpretation of these error components remains consistent between
the
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
penalization cases, there are notable differences.
The first key difference is that
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with
\ell_{1}
penalization is
applicable in more general settings. The moment conditions required in
Theorem
1
are weaker than those in
Theorem
2
. Specifically, for
\ell_{1}
penalization, the error
term
\varepsilon_{\Xi}
can be heavy-tailed, whereas
\ell_{2}
penalization
requires
\varepsilon_{\Xi}
to be light-tailed enough to have a finite second
moment. As a result,
\ell_{1}
penalization can handle external data with
contamination and heavy-tailed errors, while
\ell_{2}
penalization may struggle
under such circumstances due to its stricter distributional assumptions.
Another key difference lies in the term related to the bias

I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}
. In Theorem
2
, this term becomes

s_{\gamma,0}=n_{\Xi}^{-1}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{O}}|\gamma_{e}|\approx p_{\gamma}\mathbb{E}(|\tilde{\gamma}|)
. To ensure this term goes toward zero, both
the proportion of the outliers
p_{\gamma}
and the magnitude of the bias terms must remain
sufficiently small, regardless of how small
p_{\gamma}
is. This limitation may impact
the performance of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with
\ell_{2}
penalization in cases where

\Xi
contains sparse outliers with large magnitudes.

3.3
Optimal Sampling Probabilities under Data Contamination

A key step of the transfer learning estimator based on
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
is determining the
optimal
\pi_{e}
. In subsampling literature, this is typically achieved by
minimizing the asymptotic variance of the subsample estimator, assuming it is
asymptotically unbiased. However, as highlighted in Section
2
,
directly applying this strategy is unsuitable in our setting due to the presence
of outliers. In this section, we determine optimal sampling
probabilities for
\Xi
under our data contamination scenario. We first present
a corollary that implies the asymptotic normality
of the weighted estimator,
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
.

Corollary 1
.

Assume that
\bm{x}_{e}
for
e\in\Xi
and
\bm{x}_{t}
for
t\in\mathcal{T}
have the same
distribution. For any
\varsigma>0
,
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
has the following
properties:

(I)

Under the conditions in Theorem
1
, if

\sigma_{\varepsilon,1}^{{\textrm{\tiny W}}}<\infty
, then the estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with
\ell_{1}

penalization and
\lambda=\{(c_{\pi}r)/\varsigma\}^{\frac{1}{2}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}-\bm{\beta}_{0})-\frac{1}{N_{f}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\varepsilon_{t}\bm{x}_{t}+\sum_{e\in\Xi}\frac{\rho\delta_{e}}{\pi_{e}}\psi_{1}(\varepsilon_{e};\lambda)\tilde{\bm{x}}_{e}\right\}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{r}{N_{f}}\check{I}_{\bm{\gamma}_{e}}+\frac{C(d+\varsigma)}{N_{f}},

with probability at least
1-Ce^{-\varsigma}
,
where
N_{f}=n_{\mathcal{T}}+r
is the fused sample size,

\psi_{1}(z;\lambda)=zI(|z|\leq\lambda)+\lambda I(z>\lambda)-\lambda I(z<-\lambda)
,

\check{I}_{\gamma}=I_{\gamma}+rN_{f}^{-1}(I_{\gamma})^{2}+(d+\varsigma)r^{2}N_{f}^{-3}(I_{\gamma})^{3}
, and
C
is a constant that
does not depend on
n_{\mathcal{T}}
and
n_{\Xi}
.

(II)

Under the conditions in Theorem
2
, the estimator

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
with
\ell_{2}
penalization satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}-\bm{\beta}_{0})-\frac{1}{N_{f\lambda}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\varepsilon_{t}\bm{x}_{t}+\sum_{e\in\Xi}\frac{\rho\delta_{e}}{\pi_{e}}\psi_{2}(\varepsilon_{e};\lambda)\bm{x}_{e}\right\}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{c_{\lambda}r}{N_{f\lambda}}I_{\gamma}+\frac{C(d+\varsigma)}{N_{f\lambda}},

with probability at least
1-Ce^{-\varsigma}
, where

\psi_{2}(z,\lambda)=\lambda z/(1+\lambda)
,
c_{\lambda}=\lambda/(1+\lambda)
, and
N_{f\lambda}=n_{\mathcal{T}}+c_{\lambda}r
is
the fused sample size adjusted by
\lambda
.

Remark 1
.

Corollary
1
implies the asymptotic normality of

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
under some conditions. Denote

\mathbb{E}[\{\rho\psi(\varepsilon_{\Xi};\lambda)\}^{2}/\pi]=\sigma_{\Xi}^{2}
.
Under some mild regularity conditions,

\sigma^{-1}_{\mathcal{T}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}}}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\varepsilon_{t}\bm{x}_{t}\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d}),\text{ and }\sigma^{-1}_{\Xi}\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{e\in\Xi}\frac{\rho\delta_{e}}{\pi_{e}}\psi_{\nu}(\varepsilon_{e};\lambda)\bm{x}_{e}\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d}),

where
\rightsquigarrow
means weak convergence and
\nu=1,2
. This shows that

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}\approx\bm{\beta}_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}}}{N}\mathbb{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}_{\mathcal{T}}\bm{I}_{d})+\frac{\sqrt{r}}{N}\mathbb{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}_{\Xi}\bm{I}_{d})+\frac{r}{N}\check{I}_{\gamma},

where
N=N_{f}
for the
\ell_{1}
penalty and
N=N_{f\lambda}
for the
\ell_{2}

penalty.
Thus, if
\check{I}_{\gamma}=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1/\sqrt{N})
, then

\sqrt{N}(\hat{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}_{0})
is asymptotic normal.

As shown in Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
, subsampling
probabilities
\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
affect the error bound by affecting

I_{\Xi}
and
I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{var}}
. These two terms can be considered
as the variance part of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
. In traditional subsampling literature, we
can choose
\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
to minimize the asymptotic variances to
obtain optimal subsamples. However, this approach may result in subsamples with
too many outliers, because the
\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
that minimize

I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{var}}
are proportional to

|\gamma_{e}|^{(1+\alpha_{1})/2}
. Therefore, we propose to find optimal subsampling
probabilities through minimizing
I_{\Xi}
, which is the variance component
contributed by the randomness of the external data. The optimal sampling
probabilities
\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
are stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 2
.

The optimal subsampling probabilities
\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
with expected
subsample size
r
that minimize

\mathbb{E}\left\{\left\|\sum_{e\in\Xi}\frac{\rho\delta_{e}}{\pi_{e}}\psi_{\nu}(\varepsilon_{e};\lambda)\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}_{e}\right\|^{2}\Big|\bm{X}_{\Xi}\right\}\quad\text{for }\nu=1,2,

are

\pi_{e}^{{\mathrm{opt}}}=\frac{r\|\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}_{e}\|_{2}\wedge H}{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\Xi}}\|\bm{\Sigma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}_{i}\|_{2}\wedge H},\text{ }e=1,2,\ldots,n_{\Xi},

where
H
is a threshold value that ensures

\pi_{e}^{{\mathrm{opt}}}\leq 1
and
\sum_{e\in\Xi}\pi_{e}^{{\mathrm{opt}}}=r
.

Our optimal sampling probabilities in Proposition
2

are the same for
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
penalization. This is
because penalization impacts only
I_{\Xi}
by modifying the thresholding
function
\psi_{\nu}(\varepsilon;\lambda)
, which is applied to the random error
terms
\varepsilon
. However, since our goal is to determine the
optimal subsampling probabilities based on the design matrix
\bm{X}_{\Xi}
, the
function
\psi_{\nu}(\varepsilon_{e};\lambda)
does not influence the optimal

\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
.

The optimal probabilities in Proposition
2
do not dependent on the
responses. This type of sampling probabilities are useful in the scenario that
responses are difficult or expensive to obtain
(
Zhang et al. 2021
)
. Our
purpose of using response-free sampling probabilities is different. We use
covariates only to build optimal
\{\pi_{e}\}_{e=1}^{n_{\Xi}}
to avoid including
outliers.

In practice,
\bm{\Sigma}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{x}_{e}\bm{x}_{e}^{\top})
is unknown. We propose to use

\sum_{e\in\Xi}\bm{x}_{e}\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}/n_{\Xi}
to approximate it. The resulting approximate optimal
subsampling probabilities are

\hat{\pi}_{e}^{{\mathrm{opt}}}=\frac{r\|(\bm{X}_{\Xi}^{\top}\bm{X}_{\Xi})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}_{e}\|_{2}\wedge H_{a}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\Xi}}\|(\bm{X}_{\Xi}^{\top}\bm{X}_{\Xi})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}_{i}\|_{2}\wedge H_{a}},e=1,2,\ldots,n_{\Xi},

where
H_{a}
is an approximated value of
H
. Note that

\|(\bm{X}_{\Xi}^{\top}\bm{X}_{\Xi})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}_{e}\|
’s in
\hat{\pi}_{e}^{{\mathrm{opt}}}
are the square
roots of leverage scores, which are widely used in subsampling. Our results
shows that leverage scores can be used to
minimize
I_{\Xi}
. Another interesting observation
is that a special case of Proposition
2
implies the optimality of
leverage-based subsampling for Huber regression. Specifically, if there is no

\mathcal{T}
and we only consider optimal sampling for the mean-shift model with Huber
regression, then leverage-scores produce the optimal response-free
subsampling probabilities.

4
Transfer Learning with Target-guided Data Selection

In this section, we study target-guided data selection. We first
state the procedure of data selection and propose a transfer learning estimator
based on the selected data in Section
4.1
. Next, we present theoretical analysis
of the proposed estimator and discuss its implications in Section
4.2
.

4.1
Target-guided Data Selection and the Estimator

We propose a target-guided selection approach aimed at selecting
data points in
\Xi
where
\gamma_{e}
is either zero or close to zero, i.e.,
data points that share a similar underlying model with
(y_{t},\bm{x}_{t})
. Given that
the target data
\mathcal{T}
is unbiased, we use the magnitude
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|
to estimate the bias term
\gamma_{e}
in the external data.
Heuristically,
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|
serves as an estimator of
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}_{0}|=|\varepsilon_{e}+\gamma_{e}|
. Since the residuals

\varepsilon_{e}
are expected to have a distribution centered around zero,
smaller values of
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}_{0}|
typically correspond to smaller values
of
\gamma_{e}
, i.e., the “good” data points. Following this intuition, by
sorting the data in an increasing order of
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|
, we should
observe a concentration of the “good” data points at the beginning of the sorted
list. The target-guided selection is formalized in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

Target-guided selection

1:

Compute
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}:=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{\beta}}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}
;

2:

Compute
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|
, for
e=1,2,\ldots,n_{\Xi}
;

3:

Include the
r
data
points with the smallest values of
|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|
in the
subsample.

Algorithm
2
does not introduce additional randomness; the selected
data points are deterministic with given target and external datasets. The use
of extreme statistics for data selection is common in the subsampling
literature, such as
Wang et al. 2019
and

Cheng et al. 2020
, which are
driven by optimal experimental design principles, aiming to maximize the
information within the subsample. However, our motivation for using order statistics
is different: we seek to minimize bias
in the subsample by carefully selecting data points with minimal external bias.

Let the target-guided subsample be

\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}=\{e\in\Xi:|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|\leq|y-\bm{x}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|_{(r)}\}
,
where
|y-\bm{x}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|_{(r)}
is the
r
-th order statistics of

|y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}|
for
e\in\Xi
. We
define a transfer learning estimator by taking
\Xi^{*}=\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
,
w_{e}=1
and

P(\gamma_{e})=2\nu^{-1}|\gamma_{e}|^{\nu}
in (
3
), which results in the following estimator:

(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}^{\top},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}^{\top})^{\top}=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}}\left\{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}-\gamma_{e})^{2}+\lambda\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}\frac{2|\gamma_{e}|^{\nu}}{\nu}\right\}.

(14)

Again, we know from
Proposition
1
that
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
in (
14
) minimizes

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}}w_{e}\mathcal{H}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta};\lambda)=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}\mathcal{H}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta};\lambda).

4.2
Theoretical Analysis

We investigate the theoretical aspects of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
in this section,
beginning with an additional assumption.

Assumption 5
.

The density function of
\varepsilon
in
\Xi
,
f_{\varepsilon}(z)
, is
Liptchitz with a Liptchitz constant
L_{\varepsilon}
. In addition, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
, there
exists an
\alpha_{\varepsilon}>0
and a bounded function
H(z)
such that
\lim_{w\to 0}w^{-\alpha_{\varepsilon}}\{f_{\varepsilon}(z+w)-f_{\varepsilon}(z)\}=H(z)
.

Assumption
5
imposes a smoothness condition on the density of

\varepsilon
for the external data, which is essential for deriving non-asymptotic bounds for
estimators based on target-guided selection. This assumption is satisfied by
many symmetric distributions, including the normal distribution and the

t
-distribution.

Recall that we select data points in
\Xi
corresponding to the
r
smallest

\lvert y-\bm{x}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}\rvert_{(e)}
’s, which are the order statistics
of
\lvert y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}\rvert
’s given
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{T}}
, for

e=1,2,\ldots,n_{\Xi}
. Here,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{T}}
represents
\{(y_{t},\bm{x}_{t})\}_{t\in\mathcal{T}}
. We first
analyze the properties of the bias terms
(mean-shift parameters) for the selected data points. Denote the bias terms in
the selected data points corresponding to the order statistics
\lvert y-\bm{x}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}\rvert_{(i)}
as
\gamma_{[i]}
, for
i=1,2,\ldots,r
. Here,
\gamma_{[i]}
’s are the concomitants of the order statistics

(
Yang 1977
;
Chu et al. 1999
, see,)
. We will use tools for concomitants
and order statistics in extreme statistics to derive the properties of

\gamma_{[i]}
’s. We begin by presenting the following result.

Proposition 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
2
and
4
-
5
, let
p_{\gamma}
be upper
bounded away from 1 and
\tilde{\gamma}
be lower bounded away from 0, e.g.,

\tilde{\gamma}\geq\gamma_{b}>0
almost surely for some
\gamma_{b}
.
For every
\varsigma>0
, if
n_{\mathcal{T}}\gtrsim e^{2\varsigma}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\gamma_{[i]}=0,\;1\leq i\leq r\right)\geq 1-Ce^{-\varsigma}-\check{p},

where

\check{p}=\frac{Cr^{2}}{n_{\Xi}^{\alpha_{\varepsilon}\wedge 1}}+Crp_{\gamma}\mathbb{E}\{f_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\gamma})\}+\frac{Crp_{\gamma}\sqrt{\varsigma}}{\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}}},

and
C
is a constant that does not depend on the sample sizes.

Proposition
3
shows that if
p_{\gamma}<1
, meaning that there
are data points with no biases, then the probability that
Algorithm
2
selects
r
data points with no biases approaches one.

The
\check{p}
in Proposition
3
contains three terms. The first term is
the gap between the distribution of order statistics and their limiting
distribution
(
Falk and Marohn 1993
, see)
. This term converges to zero if

r^{2}=o(n_{\Xi}^{\alpha_{\varepsilon}\wedge 1})
, which can be satisfied when the
external data are much larger than the data to be transferred. Here, the

\alpha_{\varepsilon}
is determined by the
smoothness of
f_{\varepsilon}(z)
, the density function of
\varepsilon
. For
example, if
f_{\varepsilon}(z)
has a bounded derivative, then
Assumption
5
holds with
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=1
, which gives
the fastest convergence rate of
r^{2}/n_{\Xi}
.

The second term in
\check{p}
is affected by the subsample size, the tail
behavior of
\varepsilon
, and the magnitude of biases in
\Xi
. We illustrate
this by assuming
\varepsilon\sim t_{\nu}
, the
t
-distribution with degree of
freedom
\nu
. When
f_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
is the
density function of
t_{\nu}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\{f_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\gamma})\}\lesssim 1/\gamma_{b}^{\nu}
because of

\tilde{\gamma}\geq\gamma_{b}
assumed in Assumption
4
. Therefore, the
second term
rp_{\gamma}\mathbb{E}\{f_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\gamma})\}\lesssim rp_{\gamma}/\gamma_{b}^{\nu}
, indicating
that the sample size
r
that can be taken from the external data is
determined by the tail of
\varepsilon
(reflected by
\nu
) and the magnitude of
\gamma_{b}
. When

p_{\gamma}\to 0
, we can take
r=O(\gamma_{b}^{\nu})
to let this term converge to 0. If

\varepsilon
is lighter-tailed (
\nu
is larger) and
\gamma_{b}
is larger, then

r
can be larger, meaning that we can take more data points with no
biases. This is intuitive because if
\varepsilon
’s are small and

\gamma
’s are large in
\Xi
, it will be easier to identify data points with
biases.

The third term in
\check{p}
is contributed by the target-guided
selection. Here,
rp_{\gamma}\sqrt{\varsigma}/\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}}
can be viewed as the level of uncertainty caused by target-guided
selection, and it shows that using the target data
benefits the quality of the data selection. If we
use uniform sampling to take
r
data points from
\Xi
without consulting the
target data, the probability of selecting at least one biased data point
is of rate
1-(1-p_{\gamma})^{r}\approx rp_{\gamma}
, instead of
rp_{\gamma}\sqrt{\varsigma}/\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}}
. As
long as
\varsigma<n_{\mathcal{T}}
,
the target-guided data selection
may have a lower probability to include any biased data points. This term also shows that
the size of the target data limits the size of unbiased subsample we are able to
take from
\Xi
.
For example, if
p_{\gamma}\nrightarrow 0
and we take
\varsigma=0.5\log n_{\mathcal{T}}
, we can only
take at most
r=o(\sqrt{n_{\mathcal{T}}/\log n_{\mathcal{T}}})
data points from
\Xi
without
including any biased data points.
This shows that the randomness from the target data affects
the data selection, indicating that available information in the target data
limits the information one can borrow from the outlier contaminated external data.

Proposition
3
does not rely on any specific distribution assumptions
on the error term
\varepsilon
and the covariate
\bm{x}
in
\Xi
. If we assume
normality for
\varepsilon
and
\bm{x}
in
\Xi
, we can
derive non-asymptotic bounds for
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
.
We introduce the following function to ease the presentation of the results:

Q(u)=\frac{2\sqrt{F^{-1}_{\chi_{1}^{2}}(u)}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}F_{\chi_{1}^{2}}^{-1}(u)\}}{\sqrt{2\pi}u},

(15)

where
F_{\chi_{1}^{2}}^{-1}(u)
is the quantile function of the chi-square
distribution with degrees of freedom one. Here,
Q(\cdot)
is a decreasing function,
and we will come back to it with more details after the following theorem.

Theorem 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
2
, and
4

and assume that
\varepsilon
and
\bm{x}
in the
external data are normally distributed. Let

\tilde{\gamma}\geq\gamma_{b}>0
be lower bounded away from 0.
For every
\varsigma>0
and
0<c_{0}<8
, if
p_{\gamma}<\frac{1}{1+c_{0}}
,
n_{\mathcal{T}}\gtrsim e^{2\varsigma}
,

r\leq\{1-(1+c_{0})p_{\gamma}\}n_{\Xi}
,

\gamma_{b}\gtrsim\sqrt{\log n_{\Xi}}
,
and there exists a constant

0<\alpha_{3}<1
such that
r\geq 2^{\frac{2}{\alpha_{3}}}
and

r^{\frac{\alpha_{3}}{2}}n_{\mathcal{T}}\gtrsim\varsigma\log n_{\Xi}
,
then the following hold with probability at least

1-Crp_{\gamma}e^{-\frac{\gamma_{b}^{2}}{4\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}}-Ce^{-C[r^{1-\alpha_{3}}\wedge\{r/(\log n_{\Xi})^{2}\}]}-Ce^{-C\{(c_{0}^{2}p_{\gamma}n_{\Xi})\wedge\log n_{\Xi}\}}-C\varsigma^{-1}
for some constant
C
:

(I)

The estimator defined in (
14
) with
\nu=1
satisfies that

\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}-\bm{\beta}_{0}\|_{\bm{\Sigma},2}\lesssim\frac{n_{\mathcal{T}}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}I_{\mathcal{T}}+\frac{r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}I_{\Xi}^{\mathrm{tg}},

(16)

if
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\log n_{\Xi}}
, where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&I_{\mathcal{T}}=\sigma_{\mathcal{T}}\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{n_{\mathcal{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\\
&I_{\Xi}^{\mathrm{tg}}=I_{1}^{\mathrm{tg}}+I_{2}^{\mathrm{tg}},\\
&I_{1}^{\mathrm{tg}}=\sigma_{\mathcal{T}}\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{n_{\mathcal{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}Q\left\{\left(1-\frac{1}{\log n_{\Xi}}\right)\frac{r}{n_{\Xi}}\right\},\quad\text{and}\\
&I_{2}^{\mathrm{tg}}=\frac{\sqrt{\varsigma}\{(\varsigma^{2}\log n_{\Xi})\vee(p_{\gamma}n_{\Xi})\}}{\sqrt{r}}.\end{split}

(17)

(II)

The estimator defined in (
14
) with
\nu=2
satisfies that
\forall\lambda>0
,

\|\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}-\bm{\beta}_{0}\|_{\bm{\Sigma},2}\lesssim\frac{n_{\mathcal{T}}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+c_{\lambda}r}I_{\mathcal{T}}+\frac{c_{\lambda}r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+c_{\lambda}r}I_{\Xi}^{\mathrm{tg}},

(18)

where
c_{\lambda}=\lambda/(1+\lambda)
, and
I_{\mathcal{T}}
and
I_{\Xi}^{\mathrm{tg}}
have
the same definitions as in (
17
).

The bounds (
16
) and (
18
) in Theorem
3
decreases
as the target sample size
n_{\mathcal{T}}
increases, so increasing
n_{\mathcal{T}}
always benefits
the performance of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
. If
n_{\Xi}
is
fixed, then
I_{\Xi}^{\mathrm{tg}}
is a decreasing function of
r
(remember that
Q(\cdot)
is decreasing);
this implies that
if we are always able to include “good points” into the sample,
a larger subsample leads to a better performance. This is different from the results for
random sampling in Theorem
1
, where the bias term

I_{\gamma}^{\textrm{bias}}
is an increasing function of
r
regardless of

n_{\Xi}
. However, the bound does not necessarily decrease
as
r
increases if
n_{\Xi}
also increases.
For example, if
p_{\gamma}
is bounded away from 0, then

I_{2}^{\mathrm{tg}}\approx\sqrt{\varsigma}(p_{\gamma}n_{\Xi}/\sqrt{r})
, which may increase
as
r
increases since
n_{\Xi}
may increase as well. Therefore,
it is possible that a subsample estimator outperforms the full data
estimator.

When
p_{\gamma}
is small enough, e.g.,
p_{\gamma}=O(\varsigma^{2}\log n_{\Xi}/n_{\Xi})
, we have

I_{2}^{\mathrm{tg}}\approx\varsigma^{\frac{3}{2}}(\log n_{\Xi}/\sqrt{r})
. In this case, if

r
is sufficiently large, e.g.,
r\gtrsim\varsigma n_{\mathcal{T}}(\log n_{\Xi})^{2}
,
then
I_{1}^{\mathrm{tg}}
becomes the dominating term in

I_{\Xi}^{\mathrm{tg}}
, and the function
Q(u)
plays an important role in determining
the performance of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
. This function is continuous, strictly decreasing,
and satisfies
Q(0)=1
and
Q(1)=0
(as shown in Lemma
S.8
in the
supplementary material). Therefore, when
r
is much larger than
n_{\mathcal{T}}
, e.g.,

r\gtrsim\varsigma n_{\mathcal{T}}(\log n_{\Xi})^{2+\delta}
, for some
\delta>0
, then
I_{2}^{\mathrm{tg}}

is small compared with
I_{1}^{\mathrm{tg}}
and therefore can be ignored. We have that

\frac{n_{\mathcal{T}}}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}I_{\mathcal{T}}+\frac{r}{n_{\mathcal{T}}+r}I_{1}^{\mathrm{tg}}\lesssim\sigma_{\mathcal{T}}\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{\varsigma}{n_{\mathcal{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the right-hand side is the error bound for
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
.
This means that
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
will never be worse than
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

The function
Q(\cdot)
gives a closer description on the connection between the
performance of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
and the sampling rate
r/n_{\Xi}
. We plot this
function in Figure
3
to facilitate the discussion.
When the
sampling rate
r/n_{\Xi}
is small,
u=(1-1/\log n_{\Xi})(r/n_{\Xi})
is close to zero and

Q(u)
is close to one; the error bound of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
is close to that of

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
. A heuristic interpretation is the selected
“good points” are all very close to the hyperplane

y-\bm{x}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
for a small sampling rate, and the estimator is highly affected by the
uncertainty of the target data
\mathcal{T}
. When the sampling rate
r/n_{\Xi}
increase, as shown in
Figure
3
, the values of
Q(\cdot)
drops very fast to zero when

r/n_{\Xi}
goes to one. In this case, the term
I_{1}^{\mathrm{tg}}
is much smaller than
the bound for
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
. We state this in the following corollary.

Figure 3
:
Plot of
Q(u)

Corollary 2
.

Under the same assumptions in Theorem
3
, if there exists
q>1
and
C>0
, such that

(n_{\Xi}-r+r/\log n_{\Xi})^{1-\frac{1}{q}}\leq C
, then with the same probability bound as in Theorem
3
,

I_{1}^{\mathrm{tg}}\lesssim\left(\frac{\varsigma}{n_{\mathcal{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{n_{\Xi}}\right)^{1-\frac{1}{q}}.

The condition in Corollary
2
essentially assumes that
r
is at the same order of
n_{\Xi}
.
In this case,
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
can have an order improvement over
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
. A
heuristic interpretation is more unbiased points that are away from the
hyperplane
y-\bm{x}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
are included.
These data points contains additional information to the target data
\mathcal{T}
and
thus
benefits the performance of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
.

Our results in Theorem
1
and Theorem
3
offer
further insights into other
transfer learning models in the literature. For instance,
Li et al. 2022

examines a linear model on external data with a parameter shift, expressed as

\bm{y}_{\Xi}=\bm{X}_{\Xi}(\bm{\beta}_{0}+\bm{\delta})+\bm{\varepsilon}_{\Xi}
, where

\bm{\varepsilon}_{\Xi}
is the error vector. If we define

\bm{\delta}=(\bm{X}_{\Xi}^{\top}\bm{X}_{\Xi})^{-1}\bm{X}_{\Xi}^{\top}\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi}
, then the mean-shift model in
(
2
) reduces to the parameter shift model. Theorem 1 of

Li et al. 2022
imposes a condition on their method’s performance,
requiring that
\|\bm{\delta}\|_{\tau}=o(\sqrt{\log d/n_{\mathcal{T}}})
with
\tau\in[0,1]
.
This constraint essentially
necessitates that the mean magnitude of
\gamma_{e}
is not excessively large in the
mean-shift model, which is similar to the constraints in (
10
)
and (
11
) in Theorem
1
. However, for
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
, we
do not need to put any assumptions on the magnitude of
\gamma
, if
there is a positive proportion of data points in
\Xi
that is unbiased. This is
because intuitively, as long as we have enough external data and a proportion of
identifiable unbiased points, target-guided data selection is always able to
select some unbiased points with a large probability. Therefore, the magnitude
of
\gamma
will not affect
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
, because
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
are constructed based
on unbiased data points with a large probability. This is different from the
method in
Li et al. 2022
and the random subsampling estimator in
Section
3
as they put restrictions on the mean magnitude of

\gamma_{e}
and thus fail if there are data points with extreme large
\gamma_{e}
.

5
Combined Estimators & Computational issues

In Sections
3
and
4
, we introduced two data
selection strategies: Poisson random sampling and target-guided selection. Here,
we compare the random subsampling estimation with the target-guided selection
estimator.

Heuristically, the first two terms in (
13
) for random subsampling
can be considered as the
“variance part”, and the last term can be considered as the “bias part”.
While, the error bound contributed by
\Xi
in the target-guided selection can
all be considered as “variance part”. We
see that the bias term for random subsampling is larger compared with
target-guided selection if we select data points with no biases. For random
sampling, there is no restriction on the subsample size. The
variances of
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
can be made small if we take enough external data
points. However, this may result in a large bias.

On the other hand,
although the target-guided approach can select data points with no biases, the selection
may be more reliable when
r
is small. However, when sampling rate is small,
the target-guided selection may result in large variance at the same level as

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

In summary, random sampling is tailored to reduce variance but may not
be effective in mitigating biases. Conversely, target-guided
selection focuses on reducing bias but may struggle with variance minimization.
Given these complementary strengths and weaknesses, a natural idea is to combine
the two approaches. By leveraging both methods, we may strike a balance
between bias and variance, which has the potential to yield more accurate
estimators.

In this section, we propose two methods for combining these selection strategies
and address several computational considerations to support their practical
implementation.

5.1
Combined Estimators

We propose two methods for combining the data selection
approaches. Specifically, let
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
denote the subsample obtained through
random sampling and
\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
the subsample obtained via target-guided
selection. The relative sizes of these subsamples can be determined based on
prior knowledge of the external dataset
\Xi
. For example, if the external
dataset exhibits significant bias relative to the target data, a larger
proportion of observations can be allocated to
\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
to reduce bias. On the
other hand, if the external dataset is only mildly biased, assigning a larger
sample size to
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
would be more effective in mitigating variance.

One approach to combine the two data selection approaches is to merge all data
points in the two subsamples and compute a transfer learning estimator. We refer
to this method as data combining. The resulting estimator incorporates
information from three data sources and can be viewed as a special case
of (
3
) with
\Xi^{*}=\Xi^{*}_{\mathrm{c}}=\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}\cup\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
. Specifically, the combined
data estimator,
(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{c(d)}}^{\top},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{\top})^{\top}
, is the
minimizer of the following objective function:

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}_{\mathrm{c}}}w_{e}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}-\gamma_{e})^{2}+\lambda\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}_{\mathrm{c}}}\frac{2w_{e}|\gamma_{e}|^{\nu}}{\nu},

(19)

where
w_{e}=\rho\pi_{e}^{-1}
for
e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
and
w_{e}=1
for
e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
.

Another way to combine the Poisson sampling and the target-guided selection
approaches is to aggregate the estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}
in (
5
) and
the estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}
in (
14
). We
refer this approach as estimator combining, which is defined as:

\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{c(e)}}=(2\bm{V}_{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{V}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}+\bm{V}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}})^{-1}\left\{(\bm{V}_{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{V}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}})\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}+(\bm{V}_{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{V}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}})\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}\right\},

(20)

where
\bm{V}_{\mathcal{T}}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\bm{x}_{t}\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}
,

\bm{V}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}=\rho\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}\bm{x}_{e}\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}/\pi_{e}
, and
\bm{V}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}=\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}\bm{x}_{e}\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}
.
The idea of combining estimators is widely utilized in various domains,
including distributed computing
(
Lin and Xie 2011
)
, streaming data analysis

(
Schifano et al. 2016
)
, and subsampling
Yu et al. 2020
.
In our case, the combined estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{c(e)}}
uses the target dataset

\mathcal{T}
twice, thereby assigning greater weight to data from
\mathcal{T}
. This is
equivalent to assigning comparatively smaller weights to data from the subsamples

\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
and
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
.

We provide further insights on the relationship between
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{c(d)}}

in (
19
) and
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{c(e)}}
in (
20
), by defining
another objective function:

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}_{\mathrm{c}}}w_{e}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}-\gamma_{e})^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{e\in\Xi^{*}_{\mathrm{c}}}\frac{2w_{e}|\gamma_{e}|^{\nu}}{\nu}.

(21)

Compared
with (
19
), the only difference is that (
21
) assigns
less weights to the external data points in

\Xi^{*}
. Since we have computed
(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{rs}}^{\top},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}^{\top})^{\top}
based
on
\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
and
(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{tg}}^{\top},\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}^{\top})^{\top}
based on
\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
, we
can use
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}
and
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}
to replace the

\gamma_{e}
’s in (
21
), which results in

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(y_{t}-\bm{x}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\beta})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}\frac{\rho(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}-\hat{\gamma}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*},e})^{2}}{\pi_{e}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{e\in\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}(y_{e}-\bm{x}_{e}^{\top}\bm{\beta}-\hat{\gamma}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*},e})^{2},

(22)

with
\hat{\gamma}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*},e}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*},e}
being elements of

\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{rs}}^{*}}
and
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi_{\mathrm{tg}}^{*}}
, respectively. The combined
estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{c(e)}}
in (
20
) is the minimizer
of (
22
). Therefore,
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathrm{c(e)}}
is a simplified combining
estimator that puts more weights on
\mathcal{T}
.

5.2
Computational Issues

In this section, we discuss the practical implementation for the proposed
transfer learning method. As discussed in previous sections, the proposed
transfer learning estimators can be considered as a special case of the general
estimator in (
3
) with specific subsample set
\Xi^{*}
and weights

\{w_{e}\}_{e\in\Xi^{*}}
. Penalized estimators similar to (
3
)
usually involve coordinate-descend algorithm, which conducts iteration
calculation with both
\hat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*}}
alternatively. Our method can
avoid iterations on
\bm{\beta}
by utilizing the special structure of mean-shift
models. The following Proposition
4
provides an equivalent
representation of the estimator in (
3
) that provides us a unified
framework for the implementation of the proposed estimators.
It shows that
only
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*}}
requires iterative calculations with (
23
),
while
\hat{\bm{\beta}}
can be calculated with (
24
) after obtaining

\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*}}
.

Proposition 4
.

For any
\lambda>0

and penalty function
P(\cdot)
, the estimator
in (
3
) matches the estimator obtained at convergence of
iterating

\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*},\lambda}^{(k+1)}=\Theta\left(\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{z}^{(k)};\lambda\right),\bm{z}^{(k)}=(\bm{I}-\bm{H}_{\Xi^{*}}^{w})\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{y}_{\Xi^{*}}+\bm{H}_{\Xi^{*}}^{w}\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*},\lambda}^{(k)}-\bm{H}_{\Xi^{*},\mathcal{T}}^{w}\bm{y}_{\mathcal{T}},

(23)

where
\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}=\mathrm{diag}\{\ldots,w_{e},\ldots\}
, for
e\in\Xi^{*}
,

\bm{H}_{\Xi^{*}}^{w}=\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}\bm{V}^{-1}(\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}})^{\top}
, and

\bm{H}_{\Xi^{*},\mathcal{T}}^{w}=\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{X}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\top}
; and then calculating

\hat{\bm{\beta}}^{(k+1)}=\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{X}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\top}\bm{y}_{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{V}^{-1}\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\top}\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}(\bm{y}_{\Xi^{*}}-\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*},\lambda}^{(k+1)}),

(24)

where
\bm{V}=\bm{X}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\top}\bm{X}_{\mathcal{T}}+\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}^{\top}\bm{W}_{\Xi^{*}}\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}
.

Another major issue for practical implementation of a penalized estimator is to
determine the tuning parameter
\lambda
, which is usually obtained through
cross validation. However, cross validation is not appropriate in our case,
because our major goal is to improve the estimation on the target data set,
which is often of a small sample size. Data splitting on the target data may
result in very small training and/or validating sets. This may cause inaccurate
estimation and even become infeasible for the target-guided estimator with
high-dimensional data.
Therefore, we prefer a tuning method that can be implemented without data
splitting. We adopt the idea in
She and Owen 2011
and suggest tuning

\lambda
with information criteria such as Akaike information criterion (AIC)
and Bayesian information criterion (BIC) as illustrated below.

Since we have two sources of data, the target data and the external data, we
combine the residual sum of squares (RSS) of the two sources to define
information criteria. The RSS for the source data
\mathcal{T}
is

\text{RSS}_{\mathcal{T}}=\|\bm{y}_{\mathcal{T}}-\bm{X}_{\mathcal{T}}\hat{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}
, where
\hat{\bm{\beta}}
is the estimator based
on both data.
For the external data
\Xi^{*}
, a reduced linear model can be obtained as

(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}})\bm{y}_{\Xi^{*}}=(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}})\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi^{*}}+(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}})\bm{\varepsilon}_{\Xi^{*}},

where
\bm{P}_{\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}}
is the projection matrix of
\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}
. Therefore, we define the
RSS for the external data
\Xi^{*}
as the RSS form of the reduced linear model,
namely,
\text{RSS}_{\Xi^{*}}=\|(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}}})(\bm{y}_{\Xi^{*}}-\hat{\bm{\gamma}}_{\Xi^{*},\lambda})\|_{2}^{2}
.
With the combines RSS being defined as

\text{RSS}=\text{RSS}_{\mathcal{T}}+\text{RSS}_{\Xi^{*}}
, the AIC and BIC are given as
follows:

\displaystyle\text{AIC}=m\log\left(\frac{\text{RSS}}{m}\right)+2\text{df},\quad\text{ and }\quad\text{BIC}=m\log\left(\frac{\text{RSS}}{m}\right)+\text{df}\left\{\log(m)+1\right\},

(25)

where
m=n_{\mathcal{T}}+n_{\Xi}-d
, and
df
is the degrees of freedom of the model. For

\ell_{1}
penalty,
\text{df}=\#\{e:\gamma_{e}\neq 0\}+1
, where
\#
denotes the
cardinal number of the set. For
\ell_{2}
penalty,

\text{df}=\frac{n_{\Xi^{*}}}{1+\lambda}+\frac{\lambda d}{1+\lambda}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{q_{i}}{\lambda+(1+\lambda)q_{i}},

where
q_{i},i=1,2,\ldots,d
are the eigenvalues of the matrix

\bm{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{V}_{\Xi^{*}}^{-1}\bm{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Detailed calculations of these
degrees of freedom are presented in the supplementary material.

We present a practical Algorithm
3
to implement the proposed
transfer learning estimators.

Algorithm 3

Iterative algorithm for transfer learning estimators

0:

Target data
(\bm{y}_{\mathcal{T}},\bm{X}_{\mathcal{T}})
, external data
(\bm{y}_{\Xi},\bm{X}_{\Xi})
, tuning parameter set

\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, starting value
\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi^{*}}^{(0)}
, tolerance
value
\epsilon
, and max iteration
K
;

1:

Obtain a subsample
(\bm{y}_{\Xi^{*}},\bm{X}_{\Xi^{*}})
using Algorithm
1
or

2
, and record the corresponding weights
\{w_{e}\}_{e\in\Xi^{*}}
.

2:

Compute
\bm{V}
,

,

and
\bm{H}_{\Xi^{*},\mathcal{T}}^{w}\bm{y}_{\mathcal{T}}
according to Proposition
4
;

3:

for

i=1,\ldots,n

do

4:

for

do

5:

Compute
\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi^{*},\lambda_{i}}^{(k+1)}
using (

);

6:

if

\|\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi^{*},\lambda_{i}}^{(k+1)}-\bm{\gamma}_{\Xi^{*},\lambda_{i}}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}<\epsilon

then

7:

Break;

8:

end

if

9:

end

for

10:

Compute
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\lambda_{i}}
according to (

);
